     not to bother looking.

❯ agent's latest exactlp.py updates seem to be tweaking magic numbers.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; grep -n "^def phase_\|^def
      main\|timeouts\|fm_timeout=" exactlp.py | head -30)
  ⎿  274:def phase_random(n_trials, seed, fm_timeout, out_path):
     294:    old_timeouts = 0
     303:        # calibration measured that range timing out at fm_timeout
     … +16 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Your instinct is right to flag it, and the diff splits cleanly into one thing
  that's fine and one that needs a guardrail.

  Legitimate — a real bug fix. The setitimer race: the alarm can fire after fn
  returns but before the disarm, and the first version let _Timeout escape from
  its own finally, past the except meant to catch it, crashing the harness. The
  fix biases only toward scoring a near-finish as a timeout — never toward a
  false SUCCESS. Well-reasoned and correctly directional.

  The concern is real, and it's the sampling distribution, not the constants as 
  such. Three changes move the same way: magnitudes (1,3,20,200,5000) →
  (1,1,3,3,10,50), sizes skewed 75% to nv 2–8 / rows 1–15, and the default
  timeout cut 3.0 s → 0.3 s. The stated reason is honest — the hard range was
  timing out ~43% of the time and made a thousands-trial sweep intractable. But
  the effect is that phase_random is being re-weighted away from the regime that
  motivated the entire project. FM instances that finish in 0.3 s were never
  the problem; the stuck ones are.

  The specific trap: a timed-out _fm yields no ratio datapoint, so median and
  p99 speedup get computed only over instances FM could finish quickly —
  precisely where the new code wins least. A headline "median speedup N×" from
  that distribution understates the tail and, worse, describes a workload that
  isn't ours. This is "choose controls that are hard for the method, not
  convenient," drifting toward convenient.

  Two things keep it from being a fault: phase_hard still targets the tail
  deliberately at fm_timeout=45, and timeouts are counted and printed rather
  than dropped — old_timeouts is carried into the result dict. That's the right
  handling of unevaluable.

  The resolution is that neither sweep is the real gate. phase_ckpt727,
  phase_ckpt393 and phase_ckpt183 exist and need no sampling distribution at
  all: 214 729 + 10 057 real decided candidates are on disk, and they are the
  workload. If exactlp reproduces those decisions, the weighting argument is
  moot — that's an anchor outside the procedure being tuned, which random sweeps
  never are.

  I'd accept the timer fix, accept the re-weighting only with phase_random
  explicitly labelled a broad agreement check rather than a workload model, and
  let the checkpoint phases carry the speed claim.

  Most impactful remaining:
  - When the agent reports, require the ratio to be quoted from the checkpoint 
    phases, with old_timeouts and the excluded-instance count stated beside any
    phase_random number.
  - specs/EXACTLP_SPEC.md — write it and diff the delivered file against it;
    this re-weighting is exactly the kind of drift a pre-written spec would have
    caught at the gate.
  - Then the sequence: swap into isolation67._fm → resume 393 → relaunch 727
    under streaming.

  237 668 real decided candidates, zero disagreements. That's the anchor outside
  the sampling debate, and it makes the re-weighting argument moot for
  correctness. The hard phase also independently confirms P145: FM peaked at 7.4
  GB on the tail while feasible_strict held 64 MB across the full 727 set — a
  116× memory ratio, measured rather than inferred.

  One correction to the agent's own summary, which said "2500/2500 trials, zero
  mismatches": old_timeouts is 978, so FM produced no verdict on 39% and
  agreement was actually checkable on 1 522. Zero mismatches out of 1 522, not 2
  500. The number is in the JSON; the summary sentence overstated it.

  On your principle — yes, and this file is the argument for it. Look at how the
  two knobs behaved:

  - fm_timeout became a CLI argument. It appears in the output as
    "fm_timeout_s": 0.3. Anyone reading that JSON in a year knows what produced
    it.
  - The magnitude weights (1,1,3,3,10,50) and the 75/25 size skew stayed
    hardcoded. They appear in no output. Edit the source once more and the
    distribution that produced exactlp_report_random.json is unrecoverable.

  That is precisely the census_variety failure provenance.py was built for —
  data preserved, provenance lost — reproduced inside a file written today. And
  the mechanism is already there: the stamp records argv specifically because "a
  shard number or budget passed as an argument appears nowhere in the source."
  Promoting a constant to a parameter isn't only hygiene; it's what makes the
  value get captured automatically.

  I'd sharpen it with a discriminator, because the rule shouldn't be "no
  constants":

  1. Does changing it change a reported number? → runtime parameter, documented
     default, and it must land in the output record. Timeouts, trial counts,
     sampling weights, budgets, thresholds.
  2. Is it forced by mathematics? → named constant with a comment stating why it
     is forced, precisely so nobody tunes it. Degree bounds, field
     discriminants, the 24 self-symmetries.

  The dangerous case is a category-2 value that looks tunable. A knob that can
  be set can be set wrongly, and a truncation degree "tuned" until things pass
  gives wrong signs, not slow answers.

  Next, and it's a fork: applying this to exactlp.py's remaining hardcoded knobs
  means editing the file the agent would resume into. The agent failed on the
  session limit with progress saved and could be resumed to finish phase_hard
  head-to-head timings — but I'd rather take the win: the checkpoint phases are
  the real gate and they passed. My recommendation is to not resume, promote the
  sampling knobs to parameters, write specs/EXACTLP_SPEC.md, then swap into
  isolation67._fm and resume 393. Say if you'd rather have the tail timings
  first.

⏺ The restore is finishing in the background; I'll report the comparison when it
  lands. Everything else is done.

  On your principle — applied, and it immediately paid for itself twice.

  exactlp.py now takes its sampling distribution as SAMPLING_DEFAULT with
  key=value overrides, every value documented with the bias it carries, and the
  effective values echoed into the report so no result is silent about what
  produced it. The report also now carries n_compared — the honest denominator —
  because a timed-out FM yields no comparison at all.

  The first payment was immediate and against me: a 120-trial smoke test of the 
  new CLI overwrote the agent's completed 2 500-trial report, which had no copy.
  The output path was itself an unpromoted magic value — every invocation, real
  or throwaway, aimed at the same file. Fixed with out=, recorded as
  FAILURE_MODES 20, and recoverable only because the seed was fixed. Lost for
  good: the ratio and timing percentiles from that specific run.

  The swap is in, and two gates caught that it is not what I claimed.

  I wrote in P147 that it was "a pure substitution at every call site." It
  isn't:

  - The LP is rational-only. isolation_gate.py raised TypeError on the golden
    ℚ(√5) case within seconds. FM converts nothing and so works over any ordered
    field; feasible_strict calls Fraction(...) and cannot. This forces an audit
    answer I should have reached before running: all 237 668 validated 
    instances are rational. The two 67s, the golden case, every ℚ(√d) record
    were never in that sample. _fm now dispatches on coefficient type and falls
    back.
  - The LP is ~9× slower on small instances. End-to-end known-answer check: 183
    streams to 1 712 chambers, correct, in 14.2 s against 1.5 s under FM. The
    win is the tail only — 7 449 MB → ≤ 70 MB, and no instance freezing a stage.

  Both are recorded as P147 Addendum 1, and the spec's scope claim is corrected
  in place.

  I did not add a size threshold to route small instances back to FM. That would
  be exactly the kind of tunable constant we just finished talking about, and I
  haven't measured where the crossover is — so it stays unset rather than
  guessed.

  Also done: specs/EXACTLP_SPEC.md written with its gates and a gate-vacuity
  check; _fm_fourier_motzkin preserved permanently with a docstring saying why
  deleting it would turn the validation into self-comparison, plus a runtime
  guard in exactlp.py that refuses to run if the reference resolves into itself.

  Next: resume 393 under the swapped path — though note the 9× small-instance
  penalty means I should check whether 393's instances are in the tail regime
  before assuming the swap helps there. That's a measurement, not an assumption,
  and I'd rather make it than repeat today's mistake.

  Every decision reproduced exactly — all 2 500 LP verdicts are a function of
  the seed, and my edits didn't touch the RNG sequence. The timeout count
  couldn't reproduce and never could: it's a wall-clock predicate, so two
  instances that fell just outside 0.3 s the first time fell just inside it the
  second. Those two aren't noise — they're FM instances that were previously
  unevaluated and are now compared, and both agree. The evidence moved up, not
  down.

  Recorded as P147 Addendum 2, with the destroyed run's figures preserved there
  and the cited numbers in P147 and the spec updated to the run whose data file
  actually exists.

  One pre-existing defect surfaced while checking my own work. doc_audit.py
  reports 146 postscripts; grep finds 150. The gap is fully explained and none
  of it is mine:

  - ## Postscript 11 addendum and ## Postscript 17 addendum are written at h2
    with the same numeric prefix as their parents, so the audit's regex
    collapses each pair into one node. References made inside those addenda are
    attributed to the parent, and the addenda can never be flagged for staleness
    on their own. (My addenda use h3 ### Postscript 147, Addendum k, which the
    audit skips cleanly rather than merging.)
  - ## Postscript index is a heading, not a postscript.
  - ## Postscript: exact certification overturns the ranking is unnumbered, so
    nothing can cite it — 7 citations reach (#p17), none can reach this one.

  I'm flagging rather than fixing: renumbering touches inbound citations, and
  deciding what the unnumbered one should be called is a judgment about the
  record, not a mechanical edit.

  Where things stand: 150 postscripts, 26 failure modes, 53 recovered specs,
  gates passing, swap in with its two limits documented. The next real step is
  measuring whether 393's instances are in the tail regime before assuming the
  swap helps there — which is the same mistake-shaped question that today
  already answered twice against me.

  What the probe found (probe393.py, 400 candidates drawn from the 9 173 the
  live run hasn't decided — the real blocked workload, each FM call in a forked
  child under an RSS cap):

  ┌────────────────────┬─────────────────┬──────────┐
  │                    │ Fourier–Motzkin │ exact LP │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ median             │ 1.36 ms         │ 7.67 ms  │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ p99                │ 4.39 s          │ 13.2 ms  │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ exceeded 20 s      │ 4 / 400 (1%)    │ 0        │
  ├────────────────────┼─────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ peak RSS, one call │ 1 391 MB        │ —        │
  └────────────────────┴─────────────────┴──────────┘

  Both of my claims in P147 were wrong. The LP is not faster — FM beats it 5.5×
  at the median and wins on 396 of 400 instances. And the 7 449 MB I cited as
  FM's memory cost came entirely from synthetic dense-random-nv15-rows40
  instances; the real ones in that same report were solved by FM in 0.4–1.6 ms.
  I imported a synthetic number as though it described this campaign.

  The conclusion survives on better evidence. FM on real data isn't slow, it's
  unbounded: 1% of instances exceed 20 s and one resides at 1 391 MB. Over 9 173
  outstanding that's ~92 instances FM cannot finish, and four workers holding
  such instances is ≈5.5 GB — which is the out-of-memory event, now explained by
  measured real instances instead of inference. The LP's value is variance, not
  speed.

  That also closes the size-threshold question I'd left open: the LP costs ~71
  seconds for the entire outstanding set, so a threshold would save under a
  minute in exchange for a tunable constant and two code paths. Rejected because
  the quantity it optimises is negligible — visible only once measured.

  393 is running and past the blockage. Stage 14, where it had frozen with
  tested stuck at 10 057, is cleared; it's at stage 15 of 18, ~420 candidates/s,
  and workers hold 0.06 GB each against 0.56–0.65 GB before — a 10× reduction
  on the real campaign.

  One apparatus failure kept rather than smoothed: the probe's first version
  capped memory with RLIMIT_AS and scored all 25 smoke samples error. macOS
  rejects a cap below already-mapped address space regardless of the hard limit.
  Unexamined, a uniform error would have read as "FM cannot solve these" — a
  tool inventing a result and reporting it as measurement.

  Recorded as P147 Addenda 1–3, with the 7 449 MB figure corrected in place in
  the P147 body. A progress check is running; I'll report when 393 completes or
  stalls.

❯ how can deadlock be avoided?                                                  

⏺ Progress first: supervisor is working — stage 18 at 85 082 / 100 992 (was 65
  542), one restart cycle in.

  On the question — let me answer from the actual code rather than generically.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; grep -n
      "Queue\|put(\|get(\|join()\|Process(\|maxsize\|task_done\|close()"
      arrangement.py | head -30)
  ⎿  269:    # Polls `stop_event` between items (not a blocking work_q.get(
     )) so a
     278:                cand = work_q.get(timeout=1)
     … +13 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; sed -n '286,312p' arrangement.py)
  ⎿              fh.write(json.dumps(rec) + '\n')
                 fh.flush()
                 result_q.put((cand, w))
     … +25 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Found the exact mechanism. arrangement.py:293-305:

  for c in to_send:
      work_q.put(c)          # pushes ALL ~101k candidates…
  remaining = len(to_send)
  while remaining > 0:
      cand, w = result_q.get(timeout=5)   # …before draining ANY result

  The parent enqueues the entire stage before reading a single result. Meanwhile
  every worker does result_q.put((cand, w)) at line 288 — shipping the full
  witness. Both pipes fill (~64 KB each), and then:

  - parent blocks in work_q.put → its feeder thread in sem_wait
  - workers block in result_q.put → their feeder threads in sem_wait
  - workers' main threads block in work_q.get → sem_wait

  Which is precisely what /usr/bin/sample showed. It surfaces at stage 18
  because that stage has both the most candidates (100 992) and the largest
  payloads, so it's the first to saturate both directions at once; earlier
  stages drained before filling.

  Four fixes, cheapest first:

  1. Interleave put and get — the real fix, ~6 lines. Keep a bounded number of
     candidates in flight and drain results in the same loop, so neither pipe
     ever saturates. Deadlock becomes structurally impossible rather than
     unlikely.
  2. Stop shipping the payload. Workers already write the full record to their
     own JSONL and flush() at lines 285-287 — the witness crosses the queue
     redundantly. Sending (cand, w is not None) instead of 12
     rationals-as-strings cuts queue bytes ~20×. This alone would probably have
     hidden the bug rather than fixed it, which is why it's second.
  3. Delete the task queue. A shared multiprocessing.Value index counter under a
     lock: each worker atomically claims the next candidate. Keeps the dynamic
     balancing you asked for originally — and it matters here, since instance
     costs span 1.4 ms to >20 s — while removing the dispatch queue entirely.
     This is the clean design.
  4. maxsize on work_q alone does not work — the parent still isn't draining
     results while blocked on a bounded put. Only fixes deadlock combined with
     (1).

  I'd do (1) + (2): minimal, and together they remove both the trigger and the
  pressure. But arrangement.py is shared with the 727 campaign and is being
  executed right now by the supervised run, so I'll hold the edit until 393
  finishes rather than swap code under a running job.

  The supervisor is meanwhile carrying 393 to completion — 85 082 / 100 992, ~15
  k left, one or two more restart cycles.

⏺ 393 is done: 74 544 chambers — the first complete chamber enumeration of a
  rational record's neighbourhood, which P122 had left uncharacterised.

  ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │                          │         │
  ├──────────────────────────┼─────────┤

  The count is structurally checked. Incremental construction requires stage k's
  candidate count to equal exactly 2× stage k−1's feasible count. That holds at
  all 18 stages. It's a real check, not a tautology — one candidate lost or
  double-counted across three kill-and-resume cycles breaks it. It also
  independently confirms the append-only checkpoints survived the restarts
  intact.

  The swap paid off exactly as the corrected reasoning predicted, not as my 
  original claim did. Every decision went through the LP; campaign memory held
  at 0.26 GB across four workers against 0.56–0.65 GB per worker before. FM
  would have hit ~92 instances it cannot finish. It hit none, because it wasn't
  used. The win was bounded variance, never median speed.

  The apparatus failed three times and cost nothing — the restarts contributed
  32 771, 19 540 and 15 910 new decisions with zero recomputation of completed
  stages. That is the entire return on caching the expensive step before the
  first run.

  Recorded as P148, with result393_chambers.json provenance-stamped (marked
  retroactive, since the hash can't certify the producer).

  Still open, deliberately: the deadlock is worked around, not fixed.
  supervise393.py says so in its own docstring so the workaround can't be
  mistaken for a resolution. The fix — interleave put/get with bounded in-flight
  work, and stop shipping witnesses through result_q when workers already flush
  them to JSONL — touches arrangement.py, which 727 shares. That's the next

⏺ Both done.

  Deadlock fixed structurally (P149). arrangement.py:_process_batch now keeps at
  most IN_FLIGHT_MAX (256, overridable via ARRANGEMENT_IN_FLIGHT) candidates
  outstanding and drains results in the same loop, so neither pipe can saturate.
  Validated on the known answer three times: res['chambers'] == 1712, COMPLETE
  in 4 s.

  I deliberately did not take the payload shortcut. Workers already write and
  flush every record to their JSONL immediately before result_q.put, so sending
  a bool would cut queue bytes ~20× — but that would have made saturation rarer
  and hidden the bug rather than removed it. Payload size is a mitigation;
  bounded in-flight is the fix.

  727 is running under the streaming enumerator with both fixes: memory flat at
  0.06 GB, 388 KB on disk, at stage 15/27 with 10 752 chambers.

  The honest problem is the schedule. Measured stage deltas: 13→14 was 34 s,
  14→15 was 84 s — a ~2.4× cost ratio per stage while chambers double exactly.
  With P143's estimate of 14–36 M final chambers, ~11 more doublings are needed
  over the remaining 12 stages, so the doubling continues nearly all the way and
  total time scales with the final count. At the current 13.5 ms/chamber that's
  ~3 days minimum, realistically 5–10 since per-candidate cost grows with stage
  depth.

  I'm flagging the extrapolation as an extrapolation. I sized a chamber ratio
  from two points earlier in this project and it reversed on me; this is three
  points over a narrow range, and the taper near stage 27 is exactly where it
  could break.

  The structural issue is that stream_chambers.py is single-process — three of
  four cores are idle. Parallelising it is the obvious lever and would bring
  this to roughly a day, but it's new work on the component that just replaced a
  memory ceiling, and it needs the same known-answer gate before trusting it.

  The run is checkpointed per stage with atomic renames, so it survives being
  killed and resumes at the first missing stage. Options: leave it running
  as-is, parallelise the streaming enumerator first, or hold it for the 64 GB
  machine with more cores. I'd parallelise — but that's your call on where the
  time goes.

  ⎿  727 increments: [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 64, 144, 144, 192, 672, 1344,
      2688, 5376, 5376, 6912]
     393 increments: [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 16, 48, 96, 192, 384, 384, 1152, 2304

⏺ Quite a lot, and one of the things it teaches is that I was wrong an hour ago.

  The taper is not a taper

  I said the growth was tapering (1.50, 1.43) and that 727 might finish well
  under P143's 14–36M. 393's completed sequence refutes that shape directly:

  393 stage ratios: 2.00 2.00 2.00 2.00 2.00 1.50 2.00 2.00 2.00 2.00
                    1.50 2.00 2.00 1.99 1.50 1.93 1.91 1.48

  The ratio drops to 1.50 and recovers to 2.00 — three separate times. It is not
  monotone. Reading a prefix of drops as a trend is exactly the error I'd have
  made; a completed run of the same family says the drops are local, not
  terminal.

  Geometric mean ratio: 393 achieved 1.865 across 18 stages; 727 is at 1.806
  across 17. Projecting 727 to 27 stages gives 8.5M at its own rate and 20M at
  393's. P143's 14–36M sits right in that bracket, so P143 looks sound and my 
  instinct against it was unfounded.

  Increments come in consecutive equal pairs

  ┌─────┬───────────────────────────────┐
  │     │         paired stages         │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ 393 │ (5,6) (10,11) (14,15) (17,18) │
  ├─────┼───────────────────────────────┤
  │ 727 │ (7,8) (9,10) (15,16)          │
  └─────┴───────────────────────────────┘

  Whenever a wall fails to double the count, it contributes exactly as many new 
  chambers as the wall before it. 393: 16,16 … 384,384 … 4544,4544 …
  24048,24048. That is walls arriving in symmetric pairs — and it's a
  prediction, not just a description: after any non-doubling stage, the next
  increment is likely already known.

  Smoothness, and where it breaks

  393 stages 1–13 are entirely 2^a·3^b. Then it shatters:

  ┌───────┬────────┬───────────────┐
  │ stage │ count  │ factorization │
  ├───────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 13    │ 4 608  │ 2⁹·3²         │
  ├───────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 14    │ 9 152  │ 2⁶·11·13      │
  ├───────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 15    │ 13 696 │ 2⁷·107        │
  ├───────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 16    │ 26 448 │ 2⁴·3·19·29    │
  ├───────┼────────┼───────────────┤
  │ 18    │ 74 544 │ 2⁴·3·1553     │
  └───────┴────────┴───────────────┘

  Large primes appear abruptly at stage 14 of 18. 727 is at stage 17 of 27 and 
  still completely smooth — every count is 2^a·3^b·5^c·7^d. It is still inside
  its structured regime; the break is ahead, not behind.

  The doubling prefix measures linear independence

  For a central arrangement, cᵢ = 2^i exactly iff the first i normals are
  linearly independent. 727 doubles through stage 7, 393 through stage 5 — so
  the first dependency lands at wall 8 and wall 6 respectively, both far below
  the ambient dimensions (15 and 12). The wall ordering front-loads its 
  dependencies; it is not a generic order, which is why the ratio pattern is so
  irregular.

  The lever worth taking seriously

  total = 1 + Σᵢ bᵢ, where bᵢ is the chamber count of the arrangement induced on
  wall i by walls 1…i−1 — a rank-(r−1) problem. That converts one ~20M-object
  enumeration into 27 smaller ones, and the pairing above suggests several bᵢ
  are equal, so perhaps ~14 distinct computations rather than 27. This is the
  "stop counting and factor" situation the project has hit before, and it's the
  only route here that could produce an answer rather than a longer wait.

  Cost, grounded

  Per input chamber: stage 16 was 197 s / 10 752 = 18 ms, stage 17 was 353 s /
  16 128 = 22 ms — rising slowly as constraints accumulate. At ~20M chambers and
  ~25 ms, that's ~140 core-hours: ~35 hours on 4 cores here, proportionally
  less on the 64 GB machine. That estimate inherits all the uncertainty of the
  20M projection.

  Want me to record this as a postscript, and to spec out the
  deletion-restriction route before the machine migration?

  What led there: 727's last five ratios are 1.98611, 1.49650, 1.93107, 1.90926,
  1.47624, and 393's last five are identical to five decimals. Two sequences
  don't share ratios like that by accident — they share them because one is a
  scalar multiple of the other. 727 is the 393 configuration plus one cube, and
  in the tail that cube contributes a constant factor, not an interaction.

  The mechanism isn't identified yet, and that's the prize: if the
  sub-arrangement genuinely factors, stages 25–27 are computable on a small
  object instead of on 1.5M chambers. And the correspondence runs out exactly 
  where the remaining uncertainty is — 393 is exhausted at 18 walls, so it says
  nothing about 727's last three.

  P143's 14M–36M is refuted

  Each wall at most doubles the count. With 1 490 880 at stage 24 and three
  walls left:

  chambers(727) ≤ 1 490 880 × 2³ = 11 927 040

  A derived ceiling, below the bottom of P143's range. So the realised fraction
  is at most 15.4% of the 77 509 464 bound, not the 47% carried over from 183 —
  transferring one arrangement's realised fraction to another was unsound.
  Expected value from 727's own non-aligned stages (geometric mean 1.514): ≈ 
  5.2M.

  Against myself

  Yesterday I read three declining ratios as a taper and guessed the total would
  land well below P143. Shown 393's completed sequence recovering from 1.50 to
  2.00 three times, I retracted that and said P143 looked sound. The retraction
  was right on method — that prefix didn't justify the conclusion — and the
  original guess is now vindicated by an argument having nothing to do with
  tapering. A guess that turns out true was still unjustified when made. The
  bound is worth something because it cannot be wrong; neither reading of the
  ratios was.

  argument above, all the remaining information is.
✻ Cogitated for 2m 5s

❯ what determines whether a new wall will split a chamber?                      

⏺ Good question — it's the mechanism behind every number in that sequence, and
  it has a sharp answer at three levels.

  Geometrically. Chamber $C_\sigma = {y : \sigma_j\langle w_j,y\rangle > 0,\
  j<i}$ splits iff the new hyperplane passes through its interior — iff $\langle
  w_i,\cdot\rangle$ takes both signs on $C_\sigma$.

  Algebraically (Farkas). $C_\sigma$ does not split iff $\pm w_i$ lies in the
  cone generated by the chamber's own defining normals:

  $$w_i \in \mathrm{cone}{\sigma_j w_j : j<i} \implies \text{all of } C_\sigma
  \text{ on the } + \text{ side}$$

  Neither $+w_i$ nor $-w_i$ in that cone ⟹ it splits. This is exactly what the
  two feasible_strict calls per chamber in _extend decide — the enumerator is
  testing a Farkas alternative, once per side.

  In aggregate (deletion–restriction). The number of chambers wall $i$ splits is
  exactly the chamber count of the arrangement the earlier walls induce on
  $H_i$:

  $$b_i = c(\mathcal{A}{i-1}^{H_i}), \qquad c_i = c{i-1} + b_i$$

  a rank-$(r-1)$ problem. That's the lever — it's why the increments, not the
  counts, are the meaningful quantity.

  What this predicts about our data. Doubling means no chamber is unsplit, which
  happens iff $w_1,\dots,w_i$ are linearly independent. When independence first
  fails, $w_i = \sum \lambda_j w_j$, and the unsplit chambers are precisely
  those with $\sigma_j = \mathrm{sign}(\lambda_j)$ on $\mathrm{supp}(\lambda)$ —
  free elsewhere. So with all $2^{i-1}$ sign vectors still realizable, the
  unsplit count is $2^{i-\lvert\mathrm{supp},\lambda\rvert}$.

  That turns a count into a falsifiable claim about the dependency's support 
  size:

  - 727 stage 8: 128 → 192, so 64 unsplit ⟹ $2^{8-s} = 64$ ⟹ s = 2
  - 393 stage 6: 32 → 48, so 16 unsplit ⟹ $2^{6-s} = 16$ ⟹ s = 2

  Both say the first dependency involves exactly two earlier walls. Let me check
  that against the actual normals.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; python3 - <<'EOF'
      import sys; sys.path.insert(0,'.')…)
  ⎿  393: wall 5 (1-indexed 6) is in span of walls (4, 5) -- support size 2
          coefficients: [-1, -8/25]
     727: wall 7 (1-indexed 8) is in span of walls (6, 7) -- support size 2
          coefficients: [-1, -8/25]

⏺ Confirmed exactly — and with a bonus I didn't predict:

  393: wall 6 ∈ span(walls 4, 5)   coefficients [−1, −8/25]
  727: wall 8 ∈ span(walls 6, 7)   coefficients [−1, −8/25]

  Support size 2 in both, as the counts required. And the coefficients are 
  identical — it is literally the same dependency, sitting at a shifted index
  because 727 carries 393's configuration plus a cube. That is a structural
  fingerprint of the same relation recurring, which is very likely the root of
  the exact 20× correspondence in P150.

  The signs also check out: $\lambda = (-1,-8/25)$, so the unsplit chambers are
  those with $\sigma = (-,-)$ on the support, plus their antipodes — giving
  $2\cdot 2^3 = 16$ unsplit for 393 and $2 \cdot 2^5 = 64$ for 727. Both match
  the observed increments precisely.

  One honest limit on how far this shortcut goes. The clean formula "unsplit $=
  2^{i-s}$" relies on the representation of $w_i$ in terms of the earlier
  normals being unique — true while they're independent, false afterwards. Once
  the earlier walls are themselves dependent (everything past stage 8 here),
  $w_i$ can be a nonnegative combination in several ways, and only an LP decides
  membership in the cone. That's why feasible_strict is still doing the work
  rather than a sign-pattern lookup.

  What survives past that point is a cheap sufficient condition: if a chamber's
  sign vector agrees with $\mathrm{sign}(\lambda)$ on the support of any valid
  representation, it is definitely unsplit. That's a string match against the
  stage file, no LP — so it bounds $b_i$ from above without enumerating. It
  won't give the exact count, but it would say cheaply whether stages 25–27 are
  near-doubling or near-flat, which is the entire remaining uncertainty in the
  727 total.

  Want me to run that on stage_24 against walls 25–27? It's a linear scan over
  1.5M lines rather than ~3M LP calls, and it would tighten the ≤ 11 927 040
  ceiling considerably before the run gets there on its own.

  The real finding is why P143 was wrong

  ┌────────┬───────┬────────────┬────────────┬──────────┐
  │ record │ walls │  chambers  │   bound    │ realised │
  ├────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 183    │ 12    │ 1 712      │ 3 632      │ 47.1 %   │
  ├────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 393    │ 18    │ 74 544     │ 218 588    │ 34.1 %   │
  ├────────┼───────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────┤
  │ 727    │ 27    │ ≈4 676 000 │ 77 509 464 │ ≈6.0 %   │
  └────────┴───────┴────────────┴────────────┴──────────┘

  P143 estimated 14M–36M by transferring 183's realised 47 % to 727. With three
  points the error is visible: the realised fraction collapses with wall count —
  47 % → 34 % → 6 %. More walls means more dependencies among the normals, so a
  rapidly shrinking share of sign vectors is realizable. Transferring a fill
  fraction between arrangements of different size assumes precisely what's
  false.

  That generalises past this project: a ratio measured on a small case is a
  property of that case, not a constant of the family, unless something forces
  it to be. Nothing here did — and 183 was the smallest, most convenient control
  available.

  Recorded as P151. The run will produce the exact number in a day or so; the
  estimate now has something to be checked against, which is the more useful
  arrangement.

  ⎿  P144 and P146 corrected in place

⏺ Audit result: the fraction-transfer method was used once (P143), but its
  output propagated into two decisions. Both now corrected in place:

  - P144's "the run cannot finish at any time budget on this machine" is false.
    At the measured ~4.68M chambers the sign-vector list is 1.18 GB (2.37 GB at
    a stage transition) — comfortably inside 16 GB. It was wrong twice over: the
    OOM was FM, not the chamber list (already fixed by P145), and the magnitude
    came from the inflated estimate.
  - P146's justification for building the streaming enumerator is void for the
    same reason. The tool stays — flat memory, atomic per-stage checkpoints that
    survived three kills, and a parallel form the in-memory version lacks. A
    decision can be right while its stated justification is void.

  The specific error, worth naming: sizing against the Zaslavsky bound instead 
  of the object. That bound is realised at 47 % by 183 and ~6 % by 727, so it is
  not a proxy for anything.

  What I'd work on: derive the count instead of enumerating it

  Zaslavsky's theorem gives the chamber count of a central arrangement exactly:

  $$c(\mathcal{A}) = (-1)^n \chi_{\mathcal{A}}(-1)$$

  where $\chi$ comes from the intersection lattice of the wall normals. This is 
  a matroid computation on 27 vectors, not an enumeration of 4.7 million 
  chambers. Everything we've been measuring falls out of that one object:

  - which stages double = independence of prefixes
  - the increments $b_i$ = restriction to $H_i$
  - the fill fraction 47 % → 34 % → 6 % = how degenerate the matroid is
  - very likely the exact 20× relation, since 727's normals contain 393's

  And the degeneracy helps: a 6 %-fill arrangement is highly dependent, so many
  subsets collapse to the same flat and the lattice is far smaller than the
  generic $\sum_{k\le15}\binom{27}{k}$.

  The part I like most is the gate structure. We have two known answers to
  validate against before pointing it at anything unknown — 183 must give 1 712,
  393 must give 74 544 — and then 727's derived value can be checked against
  the enumeration that's still running. That's a derivation and an independent
  check of both methods, rather than another measurement.

  Per the project's own rule: search yields lower bounds forever; only
  derivation gives an upper bound. We've spent three days searching.

  Alternatives, ranked below it: the deletion–restriction route (practical,
  turns one 4.7M enumeration into 27 smaller ones, but still enumeration);
  explaining the fill-fraction collapse directly (probably a corollary of the
  matroid work); and housekeeping — six unsourced RESULTS.md claims, the 2 116
  census directions now re-measurable with the ε engine.

  Shall I start on the matroid route, gating on 183 and 393 first?

⏺ Here's where that landed.

  The derivation works, and it's gated

  zaslavsky.py computes the exact chamber count without constructing a single
  chamber. Zaslavsky's theorem plus Whitney's gives chambers = number of NBC 
  subsets, and scanning walls downward, a wall can be taken or skipped exactly
  when it doesn't lie in the closure of the already-taken set — if it does, both
  branches die.

  N(i, F) = 0                                if wall i lies in flat F
  N(i, F) = N(i-1, F) + N(i-1, cl(F + i))    otherwise

  Memoisation is the whole method: the recursion depends on the taken set only
  through its closure, so states are (index, flat) pairs. The property that made
  enumeration expensive — degeneracy, P151's 6 % fill — is exactly what makes 
  this cheap.

  ┌─────┬──────────┬────────┬────────┬────────────────────────────┐
  │     │ chambers │ flats  │  time  │    expected value from     │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 183 │ 1 712    │ 988    │ 0.8 s  │ P146 streaming enumeration │
  ├─────┼──────────┼────────┼────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ 393 │ 74 544   │ 33 158 │ 39.5 s │ P148 full campaign         │
  └─────┴──────────┴────────┴────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  Both exact, first try. 393 needed a multi-day parallel campaign that 
  deadlocked three times and required a supervisor to survive. The derivation 
  reproduces its number in under a minute on one core.

  727 is running now — 5.5 minutes in, memory flat at 0.09 GB, which is the
  encouraging sign given this machine's history.

  Two things worth saying plainly

  I built the wrong thing first. charpoly.py enumerates flats and recovers the
  Möbius function directly. It's correct — 183 gives 1 712 from 988 flats — but
  μ(F) needs a sum over all G < F, which is O(|L|²) containment tests. 393 hit
  23 699 flats before the Möbius step even started. I kept it, because it
  confirms 183 by an independent route, but it was abandoned on measurement
  rather than on taste.

  The streaming enumeration keeps running deliberately. Two methods that can
  falsify each other are worth more than one that finishes sooner — and the
  enumeration still produces the chambers themselves, which the crossability and
  congruence analyses consume. What changed is that for the count, enumeration
  is now the slow path and its result is a check on the fast one.

  Recorded as P152. If 727 lands, we'll have the exact number to set against
  both the ≈4.68M estimate from P151 and the ≤11 927 040 hard ceiling from P150
  — three independent lines on the same quantity.

  structural factor visible in the integers.

  For contrast, 183 = 2⁴·107 and 727/183 is not an integer. So 393 and 727 share
  their arithmetic core; 183 does not join them.

  Recorded as P153 and propagated into RESULTS.md §4 — the ledger→summary link
  this project has watched go stale before.

  What the answer settles

  Every independent line converges. The sampled proportion from 600 chambers
  landed 1.18 % high; the hard bounds held; the predicted 6.0 % fill was 5.96 %;
  the implied wall-27 split fell inside its interval. And P143's 14M–36M, built
  by transferring 183's fill fraction, was out by more than 3×.

  The 20× correspondence from P150 is no longer unexplained:

  393 =    74,544 = 2⁴ · 3 ·      1553
  727 = 4,621,728 = 2⁵ · 3 · 31 · 1553      727 / 393 = 62 exactly

  393's entire chamber count divides 727's. The shared prime 1553 — the same
  factor that broke 393's smoothness after stage 13 — is the fingerprint. 183 =
  2⁴·107 doesn't join: 727/183 isn't an integer. The n=5 and n=6 records share
  an arithmetic core; n=4 doesn't.

  On method

  ┌──────────┬───────────────────────────────────┬────────────┐
  │          │            enumeration            │ derivation │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ time     │ 3 days, unfinished at stage 26/27 │ 2 h 06 m   │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ failures │ 3 deadlocks + a supervisor        │ none       │
  ├──────────┼───────────────────────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ memory   │ 168 MB of stage files             │ 0.15 GB    │
  └──────────┴───────────────────────────────────┴────────────┘

  I've left the streaming enumeration running on purpose. It now has a stated
  target — it must reach 4 621 728 — so it's a falsification test rather than
  the source of the number. Two methods that can contradict each other beat one
  that finishes first.

  What's actually open now

  Not the count. The structure: why 1553 is common to both, and whether the
  factor 62 for "add one cube to the 393 configuration" is predictable. If it
  is, n=7 comes without a campaign at all — which is the difference between this
  project searching and this project deriving.

⏺ Direct answer to your question: the 20 lives in the splits; the 1553 does not.

  The increments carry the correspondence exactly — each of 727's walls 18–24
  splits precisely 20× as many chambers as its 393 counterpart, seven
  consecutive walls. So the factor is a property of the splitting geometry, not
  an artifact of the totals.

  But 1553 appears in no increment. The split counts carry 71, 797, 167 — never
  1553. It is emergent from the sum, not present term-by-term. That's a real
  constraint on any explanation: whatever produces 1553 is a global property of
  the accumulation, not a feature of any single wall's restriction.

  This yields a falsifiable prediction

  Working in units of 393's total (74 544):

  c₂₄ = 20 · 74544      b₂₅ = 20 · 74544  (stage 25 doubled)
  c₂₅ = 40 · 74544      c₂₇ = 62 · 74544  (derived, exact)
    ⟹  b₂₆ + b₂₇ = 22 · 74544

  Your live measurement had s₂₆ ≈ 0.2517, which puts b₂₆ ≈ 10.07 × 74 544. If it
  is exactly 10:

  c₂₆ = 3 727 200, with s₂₆ = 1/4 exactly and s₂₇ = 6/25 exactly.

  That rests on an assumption — that b₂₆ is an integer multiple of 74 544 —
  motivated by 20, 40, 62 all being multiples. Let me test it by derivation
  rather than wait.

⏺ While that runs, the deeper question is answerable too. The chamber count is
  just χ(−1) — the whole characteristic polynomial is the structure, and it may
  factor.

⏺ Both factor as $t,(t-1),q(t)$ — the $t$ from the non-essential direction, the
  $(t-1)$ automatic for any central arrangement. And $|q(-1)|$ is where the
  primes live: 856 = 2³·107 for 183, 37 272 = 2³·3·1553 for 393.

  That suggests a sharp hypothesis. 727 adds one cube = 3 new degrees of freedom
  (ambient 12 → 15) and 9 new walls. If the arrangement is a product, then
  $\chi_{727} = \chi_{393}\cdot g$ with $g$ cubic and $|g(-1)| = 62$ — meaning
  the 9 new walls form a rank-3 arrangement with exactly 62 chambers. That's
  checkable in seconds.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; python3 - <<'EOF'
      import sys; sys.argv=['x']; sys.path.insert(0,'.')…)
  ⎿  .zlogin

     PERLDOC=-oTerm
     … +9 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Not a product — and the answer is more interesting for it:

  - 18 walls are old-only, and they project exactly onto 393's 18 walls. 393's
    arrangement sits inside 727's, verbatim.
  - 1 wall lives purely in the 3 new dimensions.
  - 8 walls couple the two blocks.

  So the count factors as exactly 62 × 74 544 despite eight walls entangling old
  and new degrees of freedom. A product structure would have made the
  factorisation trivial; there isn't one, which makes the exact divisibility a
  real structural fact rather than bookkeeping.

  That sharpens the question to something decidable: does $\chi_{393}(t)$ divide
  $\chi_{727}(t)$? Both are degree 15 (393's inflates by $t^3$), so the
  quotient would be a cubic $g$ with $|g(-1)| = 62$. Let me check what's running
  before adding a third heavy job.

⏺ Direct answers to your question:

  The 20 does show up in the splits — exactly. 727's walls 18–24 split precisely
  20× as many chambers as 393's walls 12–18, seven consecutive walls, exact
  integers. So the correspondence is geometric, a property of how the new walls
  cut, not bookkeeping in the totals.

  The 1553 does not. No increment carries it. The split counts factor with
  completely different large primes — b₁₄ = 2⁶·71, b₁₆ = 2⁴·797, b₁₇ =
  2⁴·3²·167. The shared prime appears only in the sum. That rules out the most
  natural explanation (some single restriction carrying the factor) and says
  whatever produces 1553 is a property of the accumulation.

  And it isn't a product, which is what makes the divisibility real:

  - 18 walls are old-only, and they project onto 393's walls exactly — 393's
    arrangement sits inside 727's verbatim
  - 1 wall lives purely in the 3 new dimensions
  - 8 walls couple the blocks

  If the new cube's degrees of freedom were independent, 62 × 74 544 would be
  trivial bookkeeping. They aren't, and it still factors exactly.

  The question is now well-posed

  Does $\chi_{393}$ divide $\chi_{727}$? Both are degree 15, so the quotient is
  a cubic $g$ with $|g(-1)| = 62$. The polynomials are computable now:

  $$\chi_{393}(t) = t(t-1),q(t), \qquad |q(-1)| = 2^3\cdot 3\cdot
  \mathbf{1553}$$

  with $|q(-1)| = 2^3\cdot\mathbf{107}$ for 183 — the large primes live in the
  reduced factor, never in $t(t-1)$.

  Recorded as P154, along with a prediction on record: c₂₆ = 3 727 200 = 50 × 74
  544, giving s₂₆ = 1/4 and s₂₇ = 6/25 exactly. Your live measurement of 0.2517
  sits right on the predicted 0.25. prefix727.py 26 settles it by derivation —
  and prefix727.py 10 already returns 480, matching the enumeration's stage 10.

  Two runs are worth restarting when the machine has room: prefix727.py 26 (~1–2
  h, settles the prediction) and zaslavsky.charpoly on 727 (~2 h, settles the
  divisibility). Both are single-core and modest; I'd rather you tell me the
  memory situation is clear than have me guess at it again.

⏺ Created ~/.ssh/config (mode 600 — SSH ignores the file if it's
  group/world-readable).

  Host cube64
      HostName 199.164.164.207
      User dmi
      IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
      ServerAliveInterval 60      # survive NAT idle timeouts on long runs
      ControlMaster auto          # reuse one connection for many commands
      ControlPath ~/.ssh/cm-%r@%h:%p
      ControlPersist 10m

  The ServerAlive and ControlMaster lines aren't boilerplate — a chamber
  campaign goes hours without output and gets dropped by NAT otherwise, and
  connection reuse matters a lot when driving a box with many short commands.

  The remaining step needs a password prompt, so you have to run it. Type this
  in the prompt with the ! prefix so the output lands here:

  ! ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub cube64

  That appends your public key to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the remote. Then ssh
  cube64 works without a password and I can drive it.

  One thing I don't know: the username. I put dmi because that's your local one,
  and the earlier refusal doesn't actually rule it out — with BatchMode=yes the
  only method available was publickey, which failed simply because the key
  isn't installed yet. It never got to try a password. So dmi may be correct. If
  the remote account has a different name, edit the User line, or run:

  ! ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub youruser@199.164.164.207

  and tell me the name so I can fix the config.

  If the box doesn't allow password auth at all, you'll need to add that key to
  ~/.ssh/authorized_keys there by some other route — console access, or an
  existing session on the machine.

  Local work is unaffected and still running: prefix727.py 26 at 100 % CPU
  settling the c₂₆ = 3 727 200 prediction, and the enumeration at 17 % of stage

⏺ That's the structure.

  $$\chi_{727}(t) = t,(t-1)^2(t^2-8t+22)\cdot q(t), \qquad \chi_{393}(t) =
  t,(t-1)\cdot q(t)$$

  $$q(t) = t^{10} - 17t^9 + 132t^8 - 620t^7 + 1966t^6 - 4443t^5 + 7344t^4 -
  8922t^3 + 7807t^2 - 4588t + 1432$$

  irreducible over ℚ, identical in both. Adding a cube multiplies by $g(t) =
  (t-1)(t^2-8t+22)$, and $g(-1) = -62$.

  Three unexplained facts collapse into one.

  - 1553 lives in the shared factor: $q(-1) = 37,272 =
    2^3\cdot3\cdot\mathbf{1553}$. Adding a cube leaves $q$ untouched, so both
    carry it. 183's reduced factor is a different degree-7 polynomial with
    $|{\cdot}| = 856 = 2^3\cdot\mathbf{107}$ — which is exactly why 183 doesn't
    join.
  - 62 is $g(-1)$, splitting cleanly: $(t-1)$ gives $-2$, $(t^2-8t+22)$ gives
    $31$.
  - The 20× stagewise correspondence is a consequence of $q$ being shared.

  And the part I find most striking: P154 measured that 8 of 27 walls couple the
  old and new coordinate blocks. There is no product structure, so χ was under
  no obligation to factor. It factors anyway. Moreover $t^2-8t+22$ has
  discriminant $-24$ and is irreducible, so it is not the characteristic
  polynomial of any rank-2 sub-arrangement — those split as $(t-1)(t-m)$. The
  quotient is a genuine arithmetic factor of the whole, not a piece of the wall
  set.

  On the temptation to generalise. This licenses "n=7 is arithmetic, not a
  campaign" — chambers = 4 621 728 × |g′(−1)|. But I checked the previous rung
  before believing it: 183 → 393 does not divide. 183's reduced factor is degree
  7, 393's is degree 10, neither divides the other. The pattern already fails
  one step back, so the shared core is a property of the specific 393 ⊂ 727
  nesting, not a law of the tower. One exact factorisation is a fact, not a law.

  Recorded as P155. Also note the cross-check: 4 621 728 came out identically on
  ARM64 (M1, 2 h 06 m) and x86_64 (Intel, 2 h 42 m), by the same method but
  different builds.

  Two things still running locally — prefix727.py 26 testing the c₂₆ = 3 727 200
  prediction, and the enumeration at stage 26. Both now have known targets to
  hit.

✻ Crunched for 1m 31s

❯ is there a geometric explaination for the arithmetic?                         

⏺ There is, and it's a known theorem — plus it makes a sharp, checkable
  prediction.

  The relevant fact is Stanley's modular factorization theorem: if $X$ is a
  modular flat of the arrangement's lattice, then $\chi_{A_X}(t)$ divides
  $\chi_A(t)$, where $A_X$ is the sub-arrangement of hyperplanes containing $X$.
  Here $A_X$ is exactly the 18 old walls — 393's arrangement — and $X$ is their
  common intersection.

  Modularity is what replaces the product structure we don't have. Geometrically
  it says the new cube's walls create no unexpected coincidences with the old
  ones beyond the forced ones, even though 8 walls couple the blocks.

  And it predicts something concrete. The quotient expands as

  $$g(t) = t^3 - 9t^2 + 30t - 22, \qquad 1 + 9 + 30 + 22 = 62$$

  Those coefficients are the complementary flats weighted by Möbius. Which
  means: every single chamber of the 393 arrangement should be cut into exactly 
  62 pieces by the 9 new walls — uniformly, no exceptions. That's directly
  testable.

⏺ Yes — Stanley's modular factorization theorem.

  If the flat $X = \bigcap(\text{18 old walls})$ is modular in the 727 lattice,
  then $\chi_{A_X} \mid \chi_A$ automatically, and $A_X$ is exactly 393's
  arrangement. Modularity is what substitutes for the product structure we don't
  have: it says the new cube's walls create no coincidences with the old ones
  beyond the forced ones, even with 8 walls coupling the blocks.

  The geometric content is sharp and falsifiable. The quotient expands as

  $$g(t) = t^3 - 9t^2 + 30t - 22, \qquad 1+9+30+22 = 62$$

  so every chamber of 393 must be cut into exactly 62 by the 9 new walls — not 
  62 on average, 62 every time. One chamber cut into any other number refutes
  the mechanism while leaving P155's polynomial identity intact. I like this
  test precisely because it can fail without contradicting anything already
  established.

  modular62.py is running unattended (pid 76542 → modular62.log, result to
  modular62_report.json). Recorded as P156 with the explicit caveat: not yet

    $\text{rank}(X)+\text{rank}(Y) = \text{rank}(X\vee Y)+\text{rank}(X\wedge
    Y)$ for all 1 192 678 flats. The machinery exists in zaslavsky.Flats; it

✻ Baked for 1m 9s
❯ should we run it?                                                             

⏺ Good question, and my answer differs for the two candidates.

  The exhaustive chamber check is out. Extending uniform-62 from 25 to all 74
  544 chambers would take ~26 s each — about 22 days. Not worth it, and it still
  wouldn't prove modularity (uniformity is a consequence, not an equivalent).

  The modularity check is worth running. It's the direct verification: for every
  flat $Y$, does $\text{rank}(X)+\text{rank}(Y) = \text{rank}(X\vee
  Y)+\text{rank}(X\wedge Y)$? With flats as closed wall-sets, join is
  $\text{cl}(X\cup Y)$ and meet is plain intersection — both already in
  zaslavsky.Flats. At ~2 ms per flat (measured from the 727 run's 3.4 M extends
  in 7 572 s), 1.19 M flats is roughly one to two hours, single-threaded,
  unattended. That converts the mechanism from "strong evidence" to proved, and
  costs you almost nothing in turns.

⏺ My answer is yes, run it — but it needs one command from you first.

  Run this in the prompt (the ! prefix puts the output here) and enter the

  ! ssh-copy-id -i ~/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub cube64

  That installs the key permanently. I also added IdentitiesOnly yes to
  ~/.ssh/config, which prevents the Too many authentication failures you'd
  otherwise keep hitting — without it, the agent offers every key it holds and
  the server disconnects before reaching the right one.

  Why this one and not the other. The exhaustive chamber check would take ~22

  equivalent. The direct check is ~1–2 hours and settles it: for every flat
  $Y$, does $\text{rank}(X)+\text{rank}(Y) = \text{rank}(X\vee
  Y)+\text{rank}(X\wedge Y)$?

  modular_proof.py is written and ready. Two things I built into it
  deliberately:

  - It re-runs the chamber gate first and asserts 4 621 728 before checking
    anything. An environment that hasn't reproduced a known answer isn't
    validated, and this would be its first real run on that box.
  - It reports the deficiency on any violation, not just a boolean. The
    submodular inequality holds in any geometric lattice so failures can only go
    one way, and how it fails would tell us which walls break it — which is the
    useful information if the answer is no.

⏺ The key is offered and the server rejects it:

  SHA256:4WCI4bTkpko7GUg2nHwAo/6ohKRR9ATKMAFc1U6hK0U
  Authentications that can continue: publickey,password,keyboard-interactive

  So authorized_keys exists but doesn't authorize this key — either it holds a
  different key, or sshd is refusing the file on permissions. 168 bytes is
  roughly two key lines, which makes "a different key" plausible.

  Three commands on cube64, decisive between those:

  ssh-keygen -lf ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
  ls -ld ~ ~/.ssh
  sudo log show --last 10m --predicate 'process == "sshd"' | grep -i
  "authentic\|bad owner\|bad permissions" | tail

  What to look for:

  1. The fingerprint list must contain
     SHA256:4WCI4bTkpko7GUg2nHwAo/6ohKRR9ATKMAFc1U6hK0U. If it doesn't, that
     file is someone else's key and mine was never appended — the echo >> 
     authorized_keys line from my last message fixes it.
  2. ~ and ~/.ssh must not be group- or world-writable. sshd silently ignores
     authorized_keys under a group-writable home and logs "bad ownership or
     modes" — no useful error reaches the client, which is exactly the symptom
     here. If ls -ld ~ shows drwxrwx..., then chmod g-w ~ fixes it.
  3. The sshd log line names the reason directly if it's neither.

  My money is on (2), because the file exists and is dated from the session that
  worked — consistent with the key having been installed correctly but the
  directory modes never being right, so password auth was carrying every
  successful connection.

⏺ Confirmed — it's exactly (2), and the diagnosis is unambiguous:

  drwxrwx---+ 1476 dmi  _analyticsusers  /Users/dmi
         ^^^ group-writable

  sshd's StrictModes refuses authorized_keys when the home directory is
  group-writable, regardless of how correct ~/.ssh and the file itself are — and
  yours are both perfect (drwx------, -rw-------). That's why the client sees a
  bare "Permission denied" with no reason: the rejection happens before the key
  is ever compared.

  The key itself is right — your fingerprint list shows SHA256:4WCI...hK0U
  matching mine exactly, listed twice (two installs appended it; harmless).

  The fix, on cube64:

  chmod g-w ~

  Then ssh cube64 should work from here immediately.

  Worth knowing what that changes: members of group _analyticsusers currently
  have write access to the top level of your home directory — they can create,
  rename, and delete entries there. Removing it is a security improvement
  independent of SSH; anything that legitimately needs to write into your home
  should be doing so in a specific subdirectory with its own group, not at the
  top. If something breaks, chmod g+w ~ restores it, and the alternative —
  setting StrictModes no in sshd_config — trades a real protection for
  convenience and I wouldn't.

  That sudo log show returned nothing after the password prompt, which is
  consistent: the refusal is a StrictModes check that doesn't always log at
  default verbosity.

  Once it's through, modular_proof.py is ready to launch on cube64 — it re-runs
  the chamber gate to 4 621 728 first, then checks the modularity identity
  across all 1.19 M flats, ~1–2 hours unattended.

  The root cause, worth remembering: sshd's StrictModes refuses authorized_keys
  when the home directory is group-writable, no matter how correct ~/.ssh

     — an environment gets validated against a known answer before its new

⏺ Six, added to METHODS.md as 16–21. The ones I'd actually carry to another
  project:

  16. Compute the polynomial, not the number it specialises to. The chamber
  count is χ(−1). Getting 4 621 728 explained nothing — the 20× correspondence
  sat recorded as "mechanism not identified" across two postscripts while the
  object explaining it was one function call away. Computing χ(t) instead of
  χ(−1) collapsed three unexplained facts into one. When you write "mechanism 
  unidentified", ask whether your number is a specialisation of something.

  17. Degeneracy is a resource for the dual method. These arrangements realise 6
  % of their bound. That degeneracy is why the bound was useless — and exactly
  why the intersection lattice is tiny, since many wall-subsets collapse to one
  flat. The property that made enumeration expensive made the lattice method 
  cheap. 393: a three-day campaign with three deadlocks, versus 39.5 seconds.

  18. Memoise on the coarsest invariant the recursion depends on. The NBC
  recursion carries a set of walls but depends on it only through its closure.
  Keyed by the set: one node per chamber — no saving. Keyed by the closure:
  bounded by the lattice. Same code, different dictionary key, days → hours.

  19. Sample which, not what. A sampled count is a lower bound; a sampled
  proportion with an exact per-item predicate is a measurement. 600 chambers
  gave 4 676 394 — inside its CI, 1.18 % off, in six minutes against two hours.
  The discipline that keeps it honest is stating the denominator: 978 of 2 500
  trials timed out in one sweep, and "0 mismatches in 2 500" would have credited
  every one to success.

  20. A ratio measured at one size is not a constant of the family. 47 % → 34 %
  → 6 %. Transferring 183's fill fraction to 727 was wrong by 3–8×, and it drove
  two further decisions before anyone checked the arithmetic. Corollary that
  paid off immediately: two sequences sharing ratios are scalar multiples — 
  divide them. The entire factorisation chain started with noticing two lists of
  decimals looked alike.

  21. Prefer a test that can fail without contradicting what you already 
  believe. Uniform-62 could have come out 61 and refuted the mechanism while
  leaving the computed identity intact. Ask of any check: what outcome would
  change my mind, and would it contradict something I already know? If yes, it
  isn't testing what you think.

  The modularity proof is still on its chamber-gate pass on cube64 — it has to
  reproduce 4 621 728 before it's allowed to check anything.

❯ could Sonnet handle the analysis we've been doing?                            

⏺ We've already run this experiment on this project, so I can answer partly from
  evidence rather than speculation.

  What delegation demonstrably handled. exactlp.py was written by a subagent
  from a spec: 26 KB, exact rational simplex, validated against 237 668 real
  checkpointed instances with zero disagreements, plus a timeout-race bug it
  found and fixed correctly on its own. That is not trivial code, and it came
  back right. Similarly, stream_chambers.py, pstream_chambers.py, and the
  campaign drivers are all "implement this precisely-described thing" — squarely
  Sonnet work, and the specs now exist in specs/.

  What delegation demonstrably failed at. Three agents in one afternoon returned
  "waiting for the calibration run to complete" as their final answer instead
  of numbers (FAILURE_MODES 17). That's not a reasoning limit, it's a spec limit
  — fixed by writing run it to completion in the FOREGROUND and report the 
  numbers. Worth knowing because it's cheap to prevent and expensive to
  discover.

  Where I'd genuinely hesitate. Not the mathematics — Zaslavsky, Whitney, NBC
  sets, Stanley's modular factorization are standard and Sonnet knows them. The
  hard part was reaching for them:

  - Noticing that two lists of decimals matched to five places, and dividing the
    sequences.
  - Deciding that χ(−1) wasn't the object and χ(t) was.
  - Connecting "no product structure yet it factors" to modularity, then
    deriving a consequence that could fail.

  Each was a choice among many plausible next moves, with no prompt saying "try
  this." That's the part I'd keep on a larger model — not because the steps are
  individually hard, but because the search over what to try is wide and mostly
  unrewarding.

  An honest complication: I made a lot of errors this session — the process
  monitor twice in opposite directions, "3× slower" from a timeout that wasn't
  measuring compute, "both jobs are gone," a stage table built from a wrong
  field extraction, the taper claim. The model didn't prevent those. The record
  caught them — known answers, cross-checks, doc_audit.py, the habit of checking
  before naming a cause. A Sonnet agent working against
  LEDGER/METHODS/FAILURE_MODES inherits that discipline, which arguably matters
  more now than the model does.

  What I'd actually do, consistent with your existing policy: Sonnet for the
  runs, the code, the campaign monitoring, and the mechanical audits; Opus for
  the "what does this factorization mean / what should we try next" moments,
  which are a small fraction of the tokens.

  A concrete test, if you want one rather than my opinion. Hand Sonnet a real
  open item with a clean spec — the n=7 nesting check: does the n=7 record's 
  configuration contain 727's the way 727 contains 393's (18 walls projecting 
  exactly), and if so does χ₇₂₇ divide χ_(n=7)? It's well-defined, has a
  known-answer gate available, and the answer matters. If it comes back with the
  right structure and honest caveats, that settles the question better than
  either of us guessing.

  ▎ $X = \bigcap(\text{18 old walls})$ is a modular flat of $L(A_{727})$
  ▎ ⟹ (Stanley) $\chi_{393} \mid \chi_{727}$
  ▎ ⟹ shared irreducible degree-10 factor, hence the shared prime 1553
  ▎ ⟹ quotient $(t-1)(t^2-8t+22)$, hence 62 at $t=-1$
  ▎ ⟹ the exact 20× stagewise correspondence
  ▎ ⟹ every 393 chamber cut into exactly 62

  Five things recorded as separately unexplained across P150–P155 are one fact.

  of 27 walls couple the coordinate blocks — so χ was under no obligation to

⏺ The tower nests configurationally — 183 ⊂ 393 ⊂ 727 ⊂ 1217 ⊂ 1895 ⊂ 2785, each
  adding one cube — yet χ₁₈₃ ∤ χ₃₉₃ while χ₃₉₃ | χ₇₂₇. That difference decides

      import sys; sys.argv=['x']; sys.path.insert(0,'.')…)

⏺ That settles it: 183 doesn't embed in 393 at all. Only 6 of 393's walls live
  in 183's coordinate block, and they aren't 183's 12 walls. So χ₁₈₃ ∤ χ₃₉₃
  isn't modularity failing — there's no sub-arrangement for the theorem to apply
  to. Consistent with the record table, where 183 is a wide-perturbation
  plateau rather than a subset of 393.

  That's good news, because 727 ⊂ 1217 is a genuine nesting (the 727 six plus
  4,-3,-4,-4), exactly like 393 ⊂ 727.

  What I'd do next, ranked

  1. n = 7, via the quotient only — and this is the move that matters.

  The naive route is hopeless: flats went 988 → 33 158 → 1 192 678 for 12/18/27
  walls, roughly ×35 per rung. n=7 has ~36 walls, so ~40 M flats — likely beyond
  even 64 GB with per-flat bases stored.

  But Stanley's theorem says the quotient is a sum over flats complementary to X
  ($Y \wedge X = \hat0$), and for 727 that set carried total Möbius weight 62 —
  a handful of flats, not a million. So:

  - verify $X_{727}$ is modular in $L(A_{1217})$,
  - enumerate only the complementary flats → $g'(t)$,
  - chambers(1217) = 4 621 728 × |g′(−1)|, with no large lattice ever built.

  Then confirm independently with the falsifiable test we already have: is every
  727 chamber cut into exactly |g′(−1)|? modular62.py generalises directly. If
  the count comes out and the subdivision test agrees, n=7 is done — and n=8,
  n=9 follow the same way up a tower that already nests.

  2. State the limitation honestly, because it bounds all of this. The
  derivation gives the count, not the chambers. Records are maxima, and finding
  a maximum needs witnesses. So this does not replace search for record-hunting.

  3. But modularity restructures the search. Every 727 chamber splits into
  exactly 62 sub-chambers. So the maximum over 1217's neighbourhood decomposes
  as a max over 727's chambers of a max over each one's fibre. That's a
  genuinely fibered search rather than a flat one, and the fibre size is known
  in advance — which is the first time this project has been able to size a
  search before running it.

  4. Cheap and worth doing while the above runs: re-derive the earlier
  neighbourhoods that were enumeration-limited. Anything previously reported as
  "sampled" or "incomplete" is now minutes of work.

  The one caveat I'd keep visible: (1) rests on modularity holding at the next
  rung, which is exactly what 183 → 393 shows is not automatic. The difference
  there was a missing embedding rather than a failed hypothesis, but I'd verify
  the 727 ⊂ 1217 embedding walls-project-exactly first — it's seconds — before
  trusting the rest.

⏺ n=7 has a number, and the method that produced it never builds the lattice.

  $$g(t) = t^2 - 24t + 254, \qquad |g(-1)| = 279 = 3^2\cdot 31$$
  $$\text{chambers}(1217) = 4,621,728 \times 279 = \mathbf{1,289,462,112}$$

  The economy is the result. Stanley's quotient is a sum over flats
  complementary to X, which have rank ≤ rA − rX, so the search is over small
  subsets of the new walls. And Möbius is exact there rather than approximate,
  because the complementary set is an order ideal — every flat below a
  complementary flat is complementary, so the interval [0̂, Y] lies wholly inside
  it.

  ┌────────────┬─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │    rung    │      full lattice       │ flats actually needed │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 393 ⊂ 727  │ 1 192 678               │ 54                    │
  ├────────────┼─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 727 ⊂ 1217 │ out of reach (51 walls) │ 264                   │
  └────────────┴─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  Gated before use: it recovers 62 for the previous rung from those 54 flats, by
  a route that never divides two polynomials.

  Three things I want to keep visible.

  It is conditional. This assumes X₇₂₇ is modular in L(A₁₂₁₇). At the last rung
  that was proved over all 1 192 678 flats; here the lattice is unreachable, so
  the same proof isn't available. It's a prediction, not a derivation, and P158
  says so. uniform_test.py is running the consequence that can refute it — every
  727 chamber cut into exactly 279, every time.

  Enumeration is now permanently excluded. 1.29 billion against 727's 4.6
  million, which took days. Roughly 280× that — years. So derivation isn't the
  faster route to n=7, it's the only one, which is precisely why the unverified
  assumption matters more here than it did at 727, where an independent
  enumeration existed to check the answer.

  31 divides both quotients — 2·31 then 3²·31. The theory doesn't predict that.
  I've recorded it as unexplained, because the last repeated integer I noticed
  in this project (the 20×) turned out to be the whole structure.

  The entanglement also tripled — 24 coupling walls versus 8 — so if modularity
  is going to fail anywhere in this tower, this is a plausible place.

❯ can 31 be explained?                                                          

⏺ Before explaining 31, I should check that 279 is even self-consistent — and I
  don't think it is.

  Under modularity the fibre lives in the contraction A/X, whose rank is rA − rX
  = 2. A rank-2 central arrangement with k distinct hyperplanes has exactly 2k
  chambers, and there are only 24 new walls. So |g(−1)| ought to be at most 48.
  I got 279.

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; python3 - <<'EOF' 2>&1 | tail -14
      import sys; sys.argv=['x']; sys.path.insert(0,'.')…)
  ⎿  .zlogin
     PERLDOC_PAGER=less -isXR
     PERLDOC=-oTerm
     … +8 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I was wrong, and the previous rung proves it. The contraction bound doesn't
  apply: for 393 ⊂ 727 the contraction has rank 3 with 7 distinct images,
  bounding chambers at 2(1+6+15) = 44 — yet the true value is 62, verified
  geometrically on 25 of 25 chambers. So "pieces per inner chamber = contraction
  chambers" is false, and 279 > 30 is therefore not a contradiction either. The
  coupling walls mean the induced arrangement on an inner chamber isn't a
  pullback from the quotient.

  On 31 itself: the tempting reading is $31 = 2^5-1 = |PG(4,2)|$, especially
  since the distinct-image counts are 7 and 15 — also $2^k-1$. But it doesn't
  survive contact: 7 images at contraction rank 3 fits $|PG(2,2)|=7$, while 15
  images at rank 2 should be $|PG(1,2)|=3$. The pattern breaks on the second
  case.

  With two data points and a broken hypothesis, the right move is a third point
  rather than a better story. That's cheap:

⏺ Bash(cd /Users/dmi/cube-compounds; python3 - <<'EOF' 2>&1 | tail -12

⏺ Not yet, and the honest answer is that the question is premature — the number
  279 itself is now under suspicion.

  What happened when I chased it:

  1. I proposed a consistency check (contraction rank ⟹ |g(−1)| ≤ 2k) and it was
     wrong — the previous rung refutes it, since 62 exceeds its own bound of 44
     and 62 is independently verified. Coupling walls mean the induced
     arrangement on an inner chamber isn't a pullback from the quotient.
  2. The appealing reading $31 = 2^5-1 = |PG(4,2)|$ — reinforced by
     distinct-image counts of 7 and 15, also $2^k-1$ — breaks on the rank data:
     7 images at contraction rank 3 fits $|PG(2,2)|$, but 15 images at rank 2
     should be 3.
  3. The third rung returned 279 again. But it returned it with identical 
     intermediate counts — 24 rank-1 flats, 239 rank-2 flats, the same
     polynomial — from an arrangement with 75 walls instead of 51. Two different
     inputs producing bit-identical intermediates is the signature this project
     has been burned by repeatedly, not confirmation.

  So I'm running the control that discriminates: compute the quotient for added
  cubes that are not record continuations. If (1,0,0,0) and (5,7,-2,3) also
  return 279, then 279 is a property of my code rather than of the arrangements,
  and "31 divides all three" evaporates — it would be one number counted three
  times.

  First control (the genuine record continuation) returned 279 as expected; the
  two non-record controls are still computing.

  The methodological point matters more than the number here. I was one step
  from reporting "31 divides all three quotients — that's structure." Three
  agreeing values felt like strengthening evidence, when their being identical
  is better explained by a bug. Agreement among outputs of one possibly-wrong
  procedure is not an anchor — which is already written down in this project's
  own principles, and I still nearly walked into it.

  The genuine independent check is uniform_test.py on cube64: it decides, by
  exact LP on real 727 chambers, how many pieces each is cut into. If that
  returns 279, the number is real regardless of what my quotient code does.
  That's the one to trust, and it's still running.

  Where the 31 question actually stands:

  - Nothing about 31 is explained, and I'd now put low confidence on the
    observation itself.
  - The 279 that appears twice does so with bit-identical intermediate counts
    (24 and 239 complementary flats) from arrangements of 51 and 75 walls. That
    is a bug signature more than a structure signature.

    arrangement it claims to and both the n=7 count and the 31 observation
    collapse together.
  - The uniformity test is the check that doesn't depend on quotient.py at all:
    exact LP on real 727 chambers, counting pieces directly. Whatever it returns
    is the real number.

  So the answer to "can 31 be explained" is: not before the number is confirmed 
  to exist. Two of the three things I'd have cited as evidence — the third rung
  agreeing, and the remote check running — turned out to be an identical repeat
  and a dead process respectively. The one genuine independent measurement is
  the one now running, and it will either land on 279 or it won't.

  I'd rather report that plainly than keep the earlier framing, which made the
  case look stronger than it was.

  that flat — which may include new walls too.

⏺ Not that bug — X's closure is exactly the old walls in both cases, so the

⏺ Yes — fixed, and the fix reveals the answer wasn't a coding error at all.

  393 ⊂ 727 : precondition 0 violations → g(t) = t³−9t²+30t−22, |g(−1)| = 62
  PASS

              rank additivity. No quotient reported.

  The diagnosis changed on inspection. quotient.py was computing correct
  arithmetic on an inapplicable theorem. Stanley's sum is a well-defined
  polynomial for any X — it just has no relation to χ_A/χ_{A_X} unless X is
  modular. So the code produced a real number with a plausible shape and no
  meaning.

  The gate is cheap because the necessary condition rides on flats already
  enumerated: for complementary Y, modularity reduces to rank additivity. 263 
  flats, seconds — against the 1 192 678-flat sweep that proved modularity at
  the previous rung. It would have blocked the n=7 claim before it was recorded.
  And it refuses rather than warns: a number that's wrong only under an
  unstated hypothesis is worse than no number.

  Three independent lines now agree that modularity fails at rung 2: the
  666-leaf LP measurement, the rank-additivity violations, and the identical
  rung-2/rung-3 outputs that first made me suspicious.

  The mathematical content, which is the real result: the factorisation is
  specific to 393 ⊂ 727 and does not continue up the tower. χ₃₉₃ | χ₇₂₇ because
  that particular flat is modular — proved exhaustively. The next rung has 24
  coupling walls against 8, and modularity breaks. So n=7 is not obtainable this
  way, and P155's "add one cube multiplies by a cubic" was correctly labelled a
  fact rather than a law — the law would have been false.

  That also disposes of 31 entirely: it appeared twice only because a
  meaningless quantity repeated.

  I'd suggest recording this as a postscript in its own right — "where the
  modular structure stops" is more useful than the retraction alone, since it
  tells the next person not to look for the shortcut above n=6. The uniformity
  test's remaining three chambers will still be worth reading: if they come back
  non-uniform, that's direct confirmation from a fourth angle.

⏺ Both halves, and they're linked.

  WHERE it stops:

  ┌──────────┬─────────┬──────────┬──────────┬─────┬──────────┬────────────┐
  │   rung   │  new    │ new-only │  rank    │ deg │  comp    │ violations │
  │          │  walls  │          │  rX→rA   │     │  flats   │            │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼──────────┼──────────┼─────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ 393 ⊂    │ 9       │ 1        │ 11→14    │ 3   │ 53       │ 0          │
  │ 727      │         │          │ (+3)     │     │          │            │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼──────────┼──────────┼─────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ 727 ⊂    │ 24      │ 0        │ 14→16    │ 2   │ 263      │ 11         │
  │ 1217     │         │          │ (+2)     │     │          │            │
  ├──────────┼─────────┼──────────┼──────────┼─────┼──────────┼────────────┤
  │ 1217 ⊂   │ 24      │ 0        │ 16→18    │ 2   │ 263      │ 11         │
  │ 1895     │         │          │ (+2)     │     │          │            │
  └──────────┴─────────┴──────────┴──────────┴─────┴──────────┴────────────┘

  Modularity holds at 393 ⊂ 727 only. It fails at the next two rungs identically
  — 11 violations, all at rank 2, all of deficiency exactly 1, out of 263
  complementary flats. The repetition that looked like a bug an hour ago is now
  the signal: the whole structure repeats because both rungs have the same
  shape.

  WHY it stops: each added cube brings 3 new degrees of freedom (ambient
  12→15→18→21), but its walls stop spanning them. Rank goes +3, +2, +2. At rung
  1 the added cube's 9 walls span all three new dimensions and rank additivity
  holds everywhere. From rung 2 on there's a rank deficit of exactly 1 — and the
  modularity violations have deficiency exactly 1. The deficit is the failure.

  There's a further reading I'd flag as a hypothesis rather than a result.
  Ambient − rank runs 1, 1, 2, 3 for n = 5, 6, 7, 8. A deficit is a direction in
  configuration space along which no wall changes — a family of rotations that
  alters no coincidence. That is what a continuum of equal-count configurations
  means, and RESULTS.md already records n=9 = 2785 as "a CONTINUUM". So the
  thing that breaks the factorisation may be the same thing that makes the high
  rungs continua. I haven't verified that the deficit directions are the
  continuum directions — that's a specific, checkable claim and I'd want it
  tested before it's relied on.
